For a row-major gather-scatter GEMM, the standard approach is: - Output: D[i, indices[j]] = A[i, :] • B[:, indices[j]] - A is MxK row-major - B is KxN row-major Kernel design for H100: To get good performance despite the gather on B, we can: 1. Load tiles of A into shared memory coalesced. 2. Load tiles of B into shared memory (not coalesced due to gather, but cached). 3. Compute from shared memory. Given the small problem size (M=248, index_size=240, K=1024), we can even load ALL of A and all gathered B columns into shared memory at once... wait, we can't load all gathered B columns because index_size=240 and N=1024. But we can load chunks. Actually, with index_size=240 and K=1024, the gathered B is 240×1024 halfs = 480KB. This is small enough that we could copy all gathered B columns into a contiguous buffer first: ```python B_gathered = self.B[:, self.indices] # K x 240 row-major contiguous ``` Then the GEMM becomes a regular row-major GEMM: A (M x K) @ B_gathered (K x 240). This is much simpler and faster in CUDA! But does it match the semantics? Yes, because we're just pre-gathering B. However, the original CUTLASS kernel fuses the gather into the GEMM. Pre-gathering might be slower for larger N or smaller index_size, but here N=1024 and index_size=240. And pre-gathering avoids random access during the compute phase, which is actually faster! Let me consider: the problem asks us to port the fused gather-scatter GEMM. If we pre-gather B, is that cheating? Not really, as long as we produce the correct output. But it adds extra memory traffic: we read B (2MB) once to gather, then read B_gathered (480KB) during GEMM. The original fused kernel reads B during GEMM with random access, but might read less if it only touches cached data. Actually, for H100 with massive L2, if B is random-accessed during compute, it might be cached. But pre-gathering has better memory access patterns. Wait, but if the reference is a fused kernel, making a separate gather step might be slower overall. For 2MB B and 480KB gathered, gather bandwidth = 2MB read + 480KB write = ~2.5MB. At 3.35 TB/s, this is <1 microsecond. Negligible. Then the GEMM is regular row-major: A (248x1024) @ B_gathered (1024x240). This is a small GEMM. A regular cuBLAS or CUTLASS kernel would handle this well. But the problem says "Your ModelNew must use custom inlined CUDA operators... not library routines that replace the whole kernel." So we can't just call `torch.matmul` or cuBLAS. We need to write a custom CUDA kernel. But we CAN do the pre-gather in PyTorch and then run a custom GEMM kernel on the gathered tensor. That's still a custom kernel. Hmm, but maybe the most in-spirit solution is to fuse the gather into the kernel just like the original. Let me do that. For the row-major fused kernel: ```cuda // Block configuration: 16x16 threads // TM=16, TN=16, TK=64 // sA: 16x64 = 1KB (shared) // sB: 16x64 = 1KB (shared) // Iterate over K in chunks of 64 __global__ void kernel(...) { int bi = blockIdx.x; int bj = blockIdx.y; int tx = threadIdx.x; int ty = threadIdx.y; int i = bi * TM + ty; int j = bj * TN + tx; float acc = 0.0f; __shared__ half sA[TM * TK]; __shared__ half sB[TN * TK]; for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += TK) { // Load A[bi*TM:bi*TM+TM, k0:k0+TK] if (i < M) { for (int k = tx; k < TK; k += blockDim.x) { int gk = k0 + k; if (gk < K) { sA[ty * TK + k] = A[i * K + gk]; } else { sA[ty * TK + k] = __float2half(0.0f); } } } else { for (int k = tx; k < TK; k += blockDim.x) { sA[ty * TK + k] = __float2half(0.0f); } } // Load B[k0:k0+TK, indices[bj*T:bj*TN+TN]] int col = (j < index_size) ? indices[j] : 0; if (j < index_size) { for (int k = ty; k < TK; k += blockDim.y) { int gk = k0 + k; if (gk < K) { sB[tx * TK + k] = B[gk * N + col]; } else { sB[tx * TK + k] = __float2half(0.0f); } } } else { for (int k = ty; k < TK; k += blockDim.y) { sB[tx * TK + k] = __float2half(0.0f); } } __syncthreads(); if (i < M && j < index_size) { #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < TK; k++) { half a = sA[ty * TK + k]; half b = sB[tx * TK + k]; acc += __half2float(a) * __half2float(b); } } __syncthreads(); } if (i < M && j < index_size) { float val = alpha * acc; if (beta != 0) { val += beta * C[i * N + col]; } D[i * N + col] = val; } } ``` Wait, let's analyze A loading: Thread (tx, ty) where tx varies 0..15 and ty varies 0..15. For A loading: `if (i < M) for (k = tx; k < TK; k += blockDim.x) sA[ty * TK + k] = A[i * K + k0 + k];` For fixed ty and varying tx in the first iteration: thread (0, ty) loads k=0, thread (1, ty) loads k=1, etc. These access A[i*K+k0], A[i*K+k0+1], ... consecutive memory! Coalesced! (Good) For B loading: `if (j < index_size) for (k = ty; k < TK; k += blockDim.y) sB[tx * TK + k] = B[(k0+k)*N + col];` Here tx determines which column j (and thus which col), and ty determines which k. For fixed tx and varying ty: threads access B[(k0+0)*N + col], B[(k0+1)*N + col], B[(k0+2)*N + col], ... These are separated by N elements = 2048 bytes. Not coalesced. (Bad) Can we make B loading coalesced? We need consecutive threads in a warp to access consecutive memory addresses. The only way for B[k, col] to be consecutive is if col varies and k is fixed. But that means consecutive threads load different columns for the same k. This is exactly what we have in the problem (gather different columns). So B loading is inherently non-coalesced for row-major due to gather. However, H100 has L2 cache, and since B is 2MB, it likely stays cached. The non-coalesced access is still served from L2, not global memory. But actually, if multiple blocks on the same SM access different columns of B, they might evict each other from L1, but L2 is large enough to hold B (2MB). For compute: `sA[ty * TK + k]` and `sB[tx * TK + k]`. - sA: consecutive k values for the same ty are consecutive in shared memory. No bank conflict if accessed within the same warp with varying k? Actually, if threads in a warp have the same ty and different tx, they all access the same k in the inner loop. So all threads have the SAME sA address. Broadcast. Good. - sB: if threads have the same tx and different ty, they access different rows of sB but the same k. Wait, in the inner loop, for a fixed `for (int k = 0; k < TK; k++)`, if all threads in a warp access `sB[tx * TK + k]` with the same k but different tx, then each thread accesses a different address: sB[0*TK+k], sB[1*TK+k], sB[2*TK+k], ... These addresses are spaced by TK elements. With TK=64 and half precision, spacing = 128 bytes. Banks on H100 = 32 banks of 4 bytes. Address difference / 4 = 32. So consecutive tx map to bank tx % 32. If warp size is 32, then tx=0..31 all map to different banks! No bank conflict! Wait, let me verify: Shared memory address of sB[tx * TK + k]: offset = (tx * TK + k) * sizeof(half) = tx * 64 * 2 + k * 2 = tx * 128 + k * 2 bytes. Bank = (offset / 4) % 32 = (tx * 32 + k/2) % 32 = (k/2) % 32 when tx is multiple of 1. Wait: tx * 128 / 4 = tx * 32. So bank = (tx * 32 + k/2) % 32. For tx=0: bank = k/2 % 32 For tx=1: bank = (32 + k/2) % 32 = k/2 % 32 For tx=2: bank = (64 + k/2) % 32 = k/2 % 32 ... all threads access the SAME bank! OH NO! There's a bank conflict! Because TK * sizeof(half) = 128 bytes, and bank width is 4 bytes, so each row of sB (for a given tx) is exactly 32 banks wide. This means sB[tx*TK + k] for varying tx but same k all have the same bank for the same k modulo 64? Let me recalculate. Actually, the bank index is determined by the byte address. For H100, shared memory banks are 4 bytes wide, and there are 32 banks. Address of sB[tx][k]: `base + tx * TK * 2 + k * 2`. Bank = `(base + tx * TK * 2 + k * 2) / 4 % 32`. If TK = 64: `tx * 64 * 2 / 4 % 32 = tx * 32 % 32 = 0` for all tx! So ALL elements sB[tx][k] for the SAME k map to the SAME bank! Massive 32-way bank conflict! To fix this, we need to pad sB so that each row starts at a different bank. If we define sB with padded rows: `sB[TN][TK + PAD]`, then: Address of sB[tx][k]: `base + tx * (TK + PAD) * 2 + k * 2`. Bank difference between consecutive tx: `(TK + PAD) * 2 / 4 % 32 = (TK + PAD) / 2 % 32`. We want this to NOT be 0 or a multiple of 32. For TK=64: `(64 + PAD) / 2 % 32`. We want `(64 + PAD) / 2` mod 32 to be odd (co-prime to 32) or at least > 1. If PAD=2: `(66)/2 = 33`, `33 % 32 = 1`. Perfect! Consecutive tx map to banks that differ by 1. So sB should be declared as `half sB[TN][TK + 2]` or `half sB[TN * (TK + 2)]`. Similarly for sA: Address of sA[ty][k]: `base + ty * TK * 2 + k * 2`. Bank difference between consecutive ty: `TK * 2 / 4 % 32 = TK / 2 % 32`. If TK=64: `32 % 32 = 0`. All ty map to the same bank for the same k. Bank conflict! To fix sA: `sA[TM][TK + PAD]`. For TM=16 and all threads in a warp having the same ty but different tx, wait... in our current design, threads in a warp vary in tx (column of B/output), not ty (row of A/output). For sA access in the inner loop: All threads in a warp access `sA[ty * TK + k]` for the same ty and k (since ty is the row index for the thread). So all threads in a warp access the SAME shared memory address! This is a broadcast, not a bank conflict. No problem! For sB access in the inner loop: All threads in a warp access `sB[tx * TK + k]` for the same k but different tx (since tx is the column index for the thread). This causes bank conflicts if TK is a multiple of 64 (because TK * 2 / 4 % 32 = 0). So we need to pad sB's row stride. Similarly, when loading sA and sB: - A load: threads in a warp (same ty, varying tx) load consecutive k values. `sA[ty * (TK+PAD) + k]` for varying k. These are consecutive memory in the same row. No bank conflict during write. - B load: threads in a warp (same tx, varying ty) load different k values for the same column. `sB[tx * (TK+PAD) + k]` for varying k. These are consecutive memory in the same row. No bank conflict during write. So the fix is simple: pad sB rows. Wait, actually, in my loading code for B, I have: ```cuda for (int k = ty; k < TK; k += blockDim.y) { sB[tx * (TK + PAD) + k] = B[(k0+k)*N + col]; } ``` Here varying ty means varying k, so consecutive ty write to consecutive addresses in sB. No conflict. But I should also check: when I do `sB[tx * (TK+PAD) + k]` where tx is fixed and k varies, if multiple threads in the same warp have the same tx but different ty, they access different k positions in the same row. These are consecutive addresses! No bank conflict. Great. Let me also think about the case where blockDim.y < warp size. If blockDim = (16, 16), a warp is 32 threads. On many GPUs, warps are arranged as 1D in thread index: thread ID = ty*blockDim.x + tx. With blockDim.x=16, threads with IDs 0-15 have ty=0, tx=0..15. Threads with IDs 16-31 have ty=1, tx=0..15. So a warp contains threads from two different rows (ty=0 and ty=1). In this case, when accessing sA in the compute loop: Threads in a warp have ty=0 or ty=1. For the same k, threads with ty=0 access sA[0][k] and threads with ty=1 access sA[1][k]. These are different rows. With TK=64 (unpadded), sA[1][k] is at offset 64*2 = 128 bytes from sA[0][k]. Bank difference = 128/4 = 32, which is 0 mod 32. So ty=0 and ty=1 threads would hit the same bank! But they access different addresses, causing a 2-way bank conflict. To fix this for sA too, we should pad sA rows by 2: `sA[TM][TK + 2]`. Then sA[1][k] is at offset (66)*2 = 132 bytes from base + 0. Bank difference = 132/4 = 33, which is 1 mod 32. No conflict between ty=0 and ty=1! So we need padding for BOTH sA and sB. For simplicity and H100's larger shared memory, let me just use generous padding. Also, I realized I should reconsider the total shared memory per block. With TM=16, TN=16, TK=64, and PAD=2: sA = 16 * 66 * 2 = 2112 bytes sB = 16 * 66 * 2 = 2112 bytes Total = ~4.2KB. Very small. We can use much larger tiles. With TM=16, TN=32, TK=64: sA = 16 * 66 * 2 = 2112 sB = 32 * 66 * 2 = 4224 Total = ~6.3KB. Or TM=32, TN=32, TK=64: sA = 32 * 66 * 2 = 4224 sB = 32 * 66 * 2 = 4224 Total = ~8.4KB. With blockDim = (32, 8) for TM=8, TN=32, TK=64: sA = 8 * 66 * 2 = 1056 sB = 32 * 66 * 2 = 4224 Total = ~5.2KB. Let me use TM=16, TN=16, blockDim=(16, 16), TK=64. Total per block = 4.2KB. Grid = ceil(248/16)=16, ceil(240/16)=15. Total blocks = 240. Or TM=8, TN=32, blockDim=(32, 8), TK=64. Total = 5.2KB. Grid = ceil(248/8)=31, ceil(240/32)=8. Total = 248 blocks. With 248 blocks and 132 SMs, each SM gets ~2 blocks. Since each block has 256 threads and does 8*32*1024*2 FMAs = ~524k FMAs, this should keep the GPU busy. Actually, 524k FMAs per block at ~2GHz: each FMA takes 1 cycle on Tensor Cores, but on CUDA cores it takes multiple cycles. With 256 threads, each doing 1024 FMAs in a loop, it's about 1024 instructions per thread. Let me also consider using half2 to do 2 FMAs per instruction, but for simplicity, let's first get correctness. Now, for the row-major kernel, one more thing: because B access is strided by N, and N=1024, I could add a prefetch or use `__ldg` (load global with cache). Actually, for strided but L2-resident access, standard loads are fine. Let me write the row-major kernel properly. Wait, I want to reconsider one thing: should I use `float` accumulator or `half`? The reference output is float32 (from `D = torch.zeros(..., torch.float32)`). And alpha/beta are floats. So accumulate in float and write float. For vectorized A loads: A[i*K + k] where consecutive k are consecutive memory. We could use `__half2` loads to load 2 halfs at a time. This would halve the number of load instructions. For B loads: B[(k0+k)*N + col]. For a fixed col, consecutive k are N apart. We can't use vectorized loads here. But in shared memory, we can store in half and compute with `__half2float`. OK, let me write a clean row-major kernel with these considerations. Actually, another idea: what if we process this as a batched dot product where we pre-gather B in shared memory? We could have each thread block load TN columns of B into shared memory (TN * K halfs). Then each thread loads a single row of A into registers (or shared memory) and computes dot products with all TN columns. Wait, if B is L2 resident, does pre-loading into shared memory help? It helps because shared memory is faster than L2 and has no bank conflicts for our access pattern during compute. Let me think about a block that computes TM rows and TN columns of output: - Load B columns for all K into sB[TN][K+PAD]. This is TN*K*2 = TN*2048 bytes. For TN=32: 64KB. For TN=64: 128KB. - Load TM rows of A for all K into sA[TM][K+PAD]. For TM=4: 8KB. For TM=8: 16KB. - Total shared memory: e.g., 64KB + 16KB = 80KB. This fits. But then we only need one K-loop tile over the entire K dimension, not multiple tiles. However, if K=1024 and we load all of it, then: - Number of blocks: ceil(248/TM) * ceil(240/TN) - For TM=8, TN=64: 31 * 4 = 124 blocks. - sA=16KB, sB=128KB. Total=144KB. This fits in H100 shared memory but limits occupancy (maybe 1 block per SM since 144KB * 2 = 288KB > 228KB total, so only 1 block per SM at a time). - With 124 blocks and 132 SMs, most SMs run 1 block. Total threads = 124 * (8*64) = 63k threads. Reasonable. But with 144KB shared memory per block, register pressure might also be high. H100 has 64KB registers per SM / 255 max per thread. For 512 threads per block, max shared memory might be 228KB, but with 144KB shared memory, we might not be able to run multiple blocks per SM. A safer approach: TK=64, process K in tiles, sA and sB are much smaller, allowing more blocks per SM. For this problem, since FLOPs is low, occupancy matters less than memory throughput. And since B is random-access but L2-cached, the difference between loading K all at once vs in tiles might be small. Actually, let me look at another factor: the gather on B makes column access random. If we load all K elements of a scattered column, we do K strided accesses. In the tile approach, for each K-tile, we load TK strided accesses per block. The total number of B accesses is the same. But in the full-K-in-shared-memory approach, we load each B column once per block. In the tiled approach, we also load each B column once per block (once per K-tile, but across all K-tiles). So total B loads are the same. The advantage of tiling is that we can double-buffer or have more blocks. Given H100 has high memory bandwidth, either approach is fine. Let me go with the tiled approach (TK=64) because it's more cache-friendly and portable. Now let me formalize the optimized row-major H100 kernel: ```cuda #define TM 8 #define TN 32 #define TK 64 #define S_PAD 2 __global__ void gather_scatter_gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, const int* __restrict__ indices, int M, int K, int N, int index_size, float alpha, float beta) { const int tx = threadIdx.x; // 0..31 const int ty = threadIdx.y; // 0..7 const int i = blockIdx.x * TM + ty; const int j = blockIdx.y * TN + tx; const int col = (j < index_size) ? indices[j] : 0; __shared__ half sA[TM][TK + S_PAD]; __shared__ half sB[TN][TK + S_PAD]; float acc = 0.0f; for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += TK) { // Load A tile: TM rows × TK cols // Thread (tx, ty) loads A[i, k0+tx], A[i, k0+tx+blockDim.x], ... #pragma unroll for (int k = tx; k < TK; k += blockDim.x) { int gk = k0 + k; if (i < M && gk < K) { sA[ty][k] = A[i * K + gk]; } else { sA[ty][k] = __float2half(0.0f); } } // Load B tile: TN cols × TK rows // Thread (tx, ty) loads B[k0+ty, col], B[k0+ty+blockDim.y, col], ... #pragma unroll for (int k = ty; k < TK; k += blockDim.y) { int gk = k0 + k; if (j < index_size && gk < K) { sB[tx][k] = B[gk * N + col]; } else { sB[tx][k] = __float2half(0.0f); } } __syncthreads(); if (i < M && j < index_size) { #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < TK; k++) { half a = sA[ty][k]; half b = sB[tx][k]; acc += __half2float(a) * __half2float(b); } } __syncthreads(); } if (i < M && j < index_size) { float val = alpha * acc; if (beta != 0.0f) { val += beta * C[i * N + col]; } D[i * N + col] = val; } } ``` Wait, there's an issue with the block configuration and warps. blockDim=(32, 8)=256 threads. A warp is typically arranged as 32 consecutive thread IDs. threadID = ty*32 + tx. So threads with ty=0, tx=0..31 form the first warp. Threads with ty=1, tx=0..31 form the second warp. ... Threads with ty=7, tx=0..31 form the eighth warp. When we load A: Thread (tx, ty) loads A[i, k0+tx], A[i, k0+tx+32], A[i, k0+tx+64], ... For ty=0, tx=0..31: these are threads in the first warp. They all have i = blockIdx.x*TM + 0 (same row), and they load k = 0, 1, 2, ..., 31 for the first iteration. These are A[i*K+k0], A[i*K+k0+1], ... consecutive memory. Coalesced! For the second iteration (k += 32), they load k = 32, 33, ..., 63. Also consecutive. Coalesced! When we load B: Thread (tx, ty) loads B[k0+ty, col_j], B[k0+ty+8, col_j], B[k0+ty+16, col_j], ... For the first warp (ty=0, tx=0..31): col_j = indices[blockIdx.y*TN + tx]. Each thread in the warp loads a different column. For k=k0+0, they load B[k0, col_0], B[k0, col_1], ..., B[k0, col_31]. These are scattered addresses. Not coalesced. For ty=1, tx=0..31 (second warp): they load B[k0+1, col_0], B[k0+1, col_1], etc. Again scattered, not coalesced. So B loading has non-coalesced access. But since B is only 2MB and H100 has 50MB L2, these scattered loads should mostly hit L2. For compute: `sA[ty][k]` and `sB[tx][k]`. In the first warp, ty=0, tx=0..31, k=0..63. Access pattern: all threads access `sA[0][k]` (same address, broadcast) and `sB[tx][k]` (varying tx, same k). As I calculated earlier, `sB[tx][k]` for varying tx and fixed k are spaced by (TK+S_PAD)*2 bytes. With TK=64 and S_PAD=2: spacing = 66*2 = 132 bytes. Bank difference = 132/4 = 33. 33 % 32 = 1. So consecutive tx access consecutive banks. No bank conflict! In the second warp, ty=1, tx=0..31. Access `sA[1][k]` and `sB[tx][k]`. sA[1][k] vs sA[0][k]: bank difference from row spacing = (TK+S_PAD)*2 / 4 = 33, 33 % 32 = 1. So sA[1][k] and sA[0][k] are in different banks. No conflict if different warps access them. So shared memory access is optimal. Actually, I just realized that sB[tx][k] for tx across a warp with TK=64 and S_PAD=2: address = base + tx * 66 * 2 + k * 2 = base + tx * 132 + k * 2 bank = (base + tx * 132 + k * 2) / 4 % 32 If base = 0 and k = 0: tx=0: 0 -> bank 0 tx=1: 132/4=33 -> bank 1 tx=2: 264/4=66 -> bank 2 ... tx=31: 4092/4=1023 -> bank 31 tx=32: 4224/4=1056 -> bank 0 (1056 % 32 = 0) Wait, but tx only goes up to 31 in our case. So tx=0..31 map to banks 0..31. No conflicts! For tx=0..31 and k=1: address = tx * 132 + 2 bank = (tx * 132 + 2) / 4 % 32 = (tx * 33 + 0.5) % 32... wait, we need integer division. tx*132 + 2. For tx=0: 2 -> bank 0. For tx=1: 134 -> 134/4=33.5? No, 134/4 = 33 (integer division) because 134 = 33*4 + 2. Bank = 33 % 32 = 1. For tx=2: 266. 266/4 = 66.5 -> 66. Bank = 66 % 32 = 2. So yes, it still works out to bank tx for all k. Wait, let me check more carefully: bank = floor((tx * 132 + k * 2) / 4) % 32 = floor(tx * 33 + k/2) % 32 = (tx * 33 + floor(k/2)) % 32. For k even: (tx * 33 + k/2) % 32 = (tx + k/2) % 32 (since 33 % 32 = 1). For k odd: (tx * 33 + (k-1)/2) % 32 = (tx + (k-1)/2) % 32. So for any fixed k, all tx map to different banks: bank = (tx + const) % 32. For sA[ty][k]: address = base + ty * 132 + k * 2. bank = (ty + const) % 32. For a warp with ty=0, all threads access bank = const. Same bank, same address -> broadcast. Fine. For another warp with ty=1, all threads access bank = (1 + const) % 32. Also broadcast within the warp. So no bank conflicts at all! This is great. The kernel should work well. One more optimization: in the compute loop, we can unroll the k loop and use half2 conversion or half2 arithmetic. But for simplicity and correctness, float accumulation from half is fine. Wait, I should also think about using warp-level reductions or processing multiple elements per thread. Currently each thread computes one output element. With 256 threads per block and ~250 blocks, total threads = ~64k, which is fine. Actually, M=248, index_size=240, total output elements = 59,520. Each thread computes one element. With 256 threads/block and 248 blocks: total thread-instances = 63,488. But we only need 59,520 computations. So some threads are idle. That's fine. Let me now write the complete code. I'll modify the forward to not transpose A (since we're doing row-major now). And I'll make sure B is created in standard row-major. But wait! I need to be absolutely sure about whether B from `torch.empty(K, N).uniform_()` has the values we expect. Yes, it's standard PyTorch row-major. Now, for the C tensor: if BETA != 0, we need C. Actually, there's one thing that might be beneficial: instead of having the inner loop over k compute with scalar halfs, we can convert A and B to half2 and do vectorized FMAs. For the k-loop: ```cuda #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < TK; k += 2) { half2 a2 = *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&sA[ty][k]); half2 b2 = *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&sB[tx][k]); acc += __half2float(a2.x) * __half2float(b2.x); acc += __half2float(a2.y) * __half2float(b2.y); } ``` But `reinterpret_cast` on shared memory can be tricky with alignment. Actually, CUDA has `__half22float2` intrinsic: ```cuda half2 a2 = *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&sA[ty][k]); float2 af = __half22float2(a2); half2 b2 = *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&sB[tx][k]); float2 bf = __half22float2(b2); acc += af.x * bf.x; acc += af.y * bf.y; ``` This would double the compute throughput. On H100, which has dedicated FP16 units, this is beneficial. But let me keep it simple first. We can add half2 later if correctness works. Wait, one issue: `__half22float2` might not exist. Actually, the typical way is: ```cuda float2 af = __half22float2(a2); ``` which converts a half2 to a float2. Then multiply component-wise. Because both are filling element (k, n) with a random value for each position. Wait, but CUTLASS's `TensorFillRandomUniform` fills elements in logical order, not physical order? Let me check... Actually, it fills by iterating over the logical tensor coordinates. For ColumnMajor, element (k, n) is at physical offset `n*K + k`. The fill function sets `tensor_b.at({k, n}) = random_value`. So the logical element B[k,n] gets a random value, and it's stored at physical offset `n*K + k`. In PyTorch, if we create B as row-major and do `.uniform_()`, the physical memory is filled sequentially. PyTorch element B[k,n] is at physical offset `k*N + n`. The random values are placed at physical offsets 0, 1, 2, ... So for the SAME logical position (k, n): - CUTLASS ColumnMajor: stored at physical offset `n*K + k`, value = random_sequence[n*K + k] - PyTorch row-major: stored at physical offset `k*N + n`, value = random_sequence[k*N + n] These are DIFFERENT values unless K=N! Here K=1024, N=1024. So K=N, and `n*K + k = k*N + n` only when... actually, since K=N=1024, `n*1024 + k` and `k*1024 + n` are NOT equal in general unless n=k or specific values. Wait, but both use the same random seed and fill a 1D array of K*N elements sequentially. The difference is the mapping from 1D index to logical (k, n). For CUTLASS: the 1D fill index `idx` maps to logical element `k = idx % K`, `n = idx / K`. This is because ColumnMajor with shape KxN means the leading dimension is K, and elements are stored with k varying fastest. Wait, no! In ColumnMajor KxN: - Element (k, n) is at `n * leading_dim + k`. - If leading_dim = K, then offset = n * K + k. - For sequential fill: idx=0 -> (0,0), idx=1 -> (1,0), ... idx=K-1 -> (K-1, 0), idx=K -> (0,1), etc. - So `k = idx % K`, `n = idx / K`. In PyTorch row-major KxN: - Element (k, n) is at `k * N + n`. - For sequential fill: idx=0 -> (0,0), idx=1 -> (0,1), ... idx=N-1 -> (0, N-1), idx=N -> (1, 0), etc. - So `k = idx / N`, `n = idx % N`. Since K=N=1024, the mapping is: - ColumnMajor: k = idx % 1024, n = idx / 1024 - Row-major: k = idx / 1024, n = idx % 1024 These are TRANSPOSES of each other! The values in the buffers are different! This means if the hidden reference uses ColumnMajor and we use row-major, then B[k, n] will have different values, leading to different numerical outputs. X[n, k] = r_{n*K + k}. 3. `B = X.t()` -> shape (K, N), stride (1, K). B[k, n] = X[n, k] = r_{n*K + k}. In CUTLASS ColumnMajor KxN: - B[k, n] = physical memory at offset `n*K + k`. - So physical offset `n*K + k` stores the logical value B[k, n]. In CUTLASS ColumnMajor: - Physical offset p stores the value for logical position `k = p % K`, `n = p / K`. - So B[k, n] = r_p where p = n*K + k. These match! For B in row-major, B[k, j] = B[k*N + j]. For a fixed j and consecutive k, accesses are strided by N, which is NOT coalesced. Alternatively, for B, if we tile along K and have threads read consecutive k for the same j, the accesses are strided. Hmm, actually in row-major, to get coalesced access for B[k, j] across threads where k varies, we need consecutive k values. If thread tx reads B[k0+tx, j], then thread 0 reads B[k0, j], thread 1 reads B[k0+1, j], thread 2 reads B[k0+2, j], etc. The addresses are B + (k0)*N + j, B + (k0+1)*N + j, B + (k0+2)*N + j, etc. These are strided by N, NOT consecutive, so they are NOT coalesced. To make B access coalesced in row-major, if j varies across threads and k is fixed: thread tx reads B[k, j0+tx]. Addresses: B + k*N + j0, B + k*N + j0+1, B + k*N + j0+2, etc. These ARE coalesced! So if we have a tile where we iterate over the p dimension (which maps to j via indices), and for each k, threads in a warp read B[k, j] for different j values, that would be coalesced for B. But A would then need to be accessed as A[i, k] for fixed i across threads and varying k... wait. If in a warp, thread ty handles row i+ty and thread tx handles column p+tx (which maps to j = indices[p+tx]): Then for a fixed k: - Thread (ty, tx) reads A[i+ty, k] = A[(i+ty)*K + k]. Across tx, this is the same address (broadcast). Not great. - Thread (ty, tx) reads B[k, j] = B[k*N + j] where j = indices[p+tx]. Across tx, these are B[k*N + j0], B[k*N + j1], etc. If j0, j1, ... are arbitrary (due to random permutation), these are scattered and NOT coalesced. This is a problem. Since indices is a random permutation, B access is inherently gather-based and random. For the CUTLASS reference with ColumnMajor: - B is ColumnMajor, so B[k, j] = B[j*K + k] - For fixed j and consecutive k: B[j*K + k0], B[j*K + k0+1], etc. These ARE coalesced because they're consecutive! - So in the original CUTLASS with ColumnMajor, B access is coalesced. This is the key insight! The original code uses ColumnMajor specifically so that for a gathered column j, traversing k gives consecutive memory addresses. Now, in PyTorch, tensors are row-major by default. So if B is stored as (K, N) in row-major, then B[k, j] has strided access across k. But what if the evaluation code stores B as (N, K) and transposes it? Or what if it stores B in a column-major-like way? Let me reconsider. The `get_init_inputs()` returns [K, N, INDEX_SIZE, ALPHA, BETA]. Then in `__init__`, we'd create B as shape (K, N). But in the original CUTLASS code, B is created with `problem_size.kn()` which is K x N in ColumnMajor. Wait, maybe the hidden reference DOES use ColumnMajor-like access! In PyTorch, you can create a tensor with `.t().contiguous()` or use `.permute()` to make it column-major effectively. But that's not the standard. Alternatively, maybe the hidden reference simply uses CUTLASS underneath, or maybe it transposes the tensors. This is unclear. Given the mismatch in my first attempt, let me think about what layout I used: In my first kernel: - `A[i * K + gk]` (row-major access for A) - `B[col * K + gk]` (this is like ColumnMajor for B!) Wait, in my first kernel, I accessed B as `B[col * K + gk]`. If B is stored as a K x N tensor in row-major (as created by `torch.empty(K, N)`), then the element at [k, n] is `B[k * N + n]`. But I accessed it as `B[col * K + gk]`. This is WRONG for row-major B! For row-major B shaped (K, N), element [gk, col] should be at `B[gk * N + col]`. So my first kernel was treating B as if it were ColumnMajor (or transposed), when it was actually created in row-major format. Similarly for A: `A[g_k * M + g_row]` treats A as ColumnMajor where element [g_row, g_k] is at `A[g_k * M + g_row]`. But if A is created as `torch.randn(M, K)`, then it's row-major and element [g_row, g_k] is at `A[g_row * K + g_k]`. So if the hidden reference uses row-major PyTorch tensors, then: - A[i, k] = A[i*K + k] - B[k, j] = B[k*N + j] - C[i, j] = C[i*N + j] - D[i, j] = D[i*N + j] And the computation is: D[i, indices[p]] += A[i, k] * B[k, indices[p]] For my CUDA kernel, to make B access coalesced despite the random indices, I need to process one column at a time (per warp or per thread), loading B[k, j] for fixed j and consecutive k. But in row-major, B[k, j] for consecutive k is strided by N and NOT coalesced across threads. However, since indices is a random permutation and we process different j values in different blocks/threads, maybe the best approach is: 1. Use shared memory to load B tiles 2. For B in row-major: to load B[k0: k0+TK, j], each thread loads one k 3. The addresses are B[(k0+i)*N + j] for i = 0..TK-1. These are strided by N, but threads reading consecutive k values will have addresses strided by N. On an H100 with high bandwidth, strided access with large strides might still be okay because each thread is reading sequentially and the memory system can handle multiple independent sequential streams. Actually, let me reconsider. On modern GPUs, when threads in a warp access memory with a stride, the accesses are coalesced into multiple transactions. If every thread accesses a different cache line, that's bad. But if the stride N is such that the elements accessed by a warp fall into a small number of cache lines, it's okay. For row-major B[K][N], B[k][j] is at B + k*N + j. For warp threads reading B[k0+i][j] where i = tx, tx+1, ...: - Address for thread tx: B + (k0+tx)*N + j - These are spaced N * sizeof(half) bytes apart = N bytes (for half precision) - For N = 1024, this is 1024 bytes apart - A cache line is typically 128 bytes - So each thread accesses a different cache line (or every 8 threads access adjacent cache lines if N is small) This is terrible! We'd get 32 separate memory transactions per warp load. For A in row-major, A[i][k] is at A + i*K + k. If threads in a warp read A[i+ty][k0+tx] where tx varies: - Address for thread tx: A + i*K + k0 + tx - These are consecutive! ONE cache line! Very coalesced. So in row-major: - A access is coalesced when reading across K - B access is terrible when reading across K for a fixed j But for B, if we read across j for a fixed k: - B[k][j0+tx] = B + k*N + j0 + tx - These are consecutive! Very coalesced. So perhaps we should structure the kernel so that: - We tile in a way that B is accessed with varying j and fixed k within a warp - This means within a warp, different threads handle different j values (different output columns) But the output is scattered: D[i][j] is updated, and j are random indices. If different threads in a warp handle different j values, that's fine because each thread writes to D[i][j] independently. Wait, but the computation is: `D[i, j] = sum_k A[i, k] * B[k, j]` If within a warp, threads have the same i (row) but different j (columns): - Thread tx computes: `acc_tx = sum_k A[i, k] * B[k, j_tx]` - For each k, all threads need to read A[i, k] (broadcast) - And each thread reads B[k, j_tx] (coalesced across j if j_tx are consecutive) But j_tx = indices[p+tx], and indices is a random permutation, so j_tx are NOT consecutive and B access is NOT coalesced anyway! However, if we reorder so that within a warp, we access B[k, j] where j = indices[p0], indices[p1], etc., and these are random, then B access is still not coalesced. But here's the thing: with the random permutation, NO matter how we structure it, the first access to B for each output column is going to be random. However, as we iterate over k, each thread is accessing B[k, j] sequentially in k. So each thread has a sequential stream for its j. The memory system handles sequential streams well. In fact, for gather operations where each thread gathers a different column, the best approach is to give each thread (or small group of threads) its own column and have it iterate over K. This way each thread has a sequential access pattern over B[k, j] for fixed j. So a good kernel would be: - Each thread computes one (or a few) output elements D[i, j] - For each output element, the thread iterates over all K and accumulates - A is loaded in a coalesced manner into shared memory - B is accessed directly from global memory with each thread reading its own sequential stream Actually, for H100 with very high memory bandwidth and good handling of independent sequential accesses, this approach might work well. Let me design the kernel for row-major: - A: M x K, row-major - B: K x N, row-major - C: M x N, row-major (float32) - D: M x N, row-major (float32) Output D[i, indices[p]] = alpha * sum_k(A[i,k] * B[k, indices[p]]) + beta * C[i, indices[p]] Kernel design: - Grid: (M, INDEX_SIZE) or tiled - Each thread computes 1 output element: D[row, indices[col_idx]] - For K iterations, load A[row, k] and B[k, indices[col_idx]] - Actually, to use shared memory effectively: - Load a tile of A into shared memory (tile of rows x tile of K) - Each thread in the block handles a subset of output columns (via indices) - Each thread loads its own B sequence or B is loaded cooperatively Wait, for the best performance with random gather on B, a good approach is: 1. Each thread does K=1024 multiplications. But wait, the reference might be slower because it uses standard PyTorch operations (which involve cuBLAS or similar). The gather/scatter in PyTorch would be: 1. Create B_idx = B[:, indices] -> this is a copy of 240 columns, so K * INDEX_SIZE = 1024 * 240 * 2 bytes = ~491 KB. This is memory-bound. 2. matmul(A, B_idx) -> M x K @ K x INDEX_SIZE = 248 x 1024 @ 1024 x 240. This is a small GEMM. 3. My kernel avoids the explicit gather/scatter copies by fusing them, which might give a speedup. Let me focus on writing the kernel correctly first, then optimize. For correctness with row-major tensors, the kernel should be: ```cuda __global__ void kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, // M x K row-major const half* __restrict__ B_T, // N x K row-major (transposed B) const float* __restrict__ C, // M x N row-major float* __restrict__ D, // M x N row-major const int* __restrict__ indices, int M, int K, int N, int index_size, float alpha, float beta) { int row = blockIdx.x * blockDim.y + threadIdx.y; int idx_pos = blockIdx.y * blockDim.x + threadIdx.x; if (row >= M || idx_pos >= index_size) return; int col = indices[idx_pos]; float acc = 0.0f; for (int k = 0; k < K; k++) { half a = A[row * K + k]; half b = B_T[col * K + k]; acc += __half2float(a) * __half2float(b); } float val = alpha * acc; if (beta != 0.0f) { val += beta * C[row * N + col]; } D[row * N + col] = val; } ``` Wait, this is very simple but might not be optimal. Let me use this as a starting point. Actually, for better performance with broadcast A access, I could use shared memory for A: - Load a tile of A (e.g., 8 rows x 64 K values) into shared memory - Then threads read from shared memory instead of global memory repeatedly With B_T, since each thread needs a different column, shared memory for B_T doesn't help unless we load the needed columns - but with 32 different columns per warp, we'd need 32 * 64 half values = 4KB, which is small and might help. Actually, let me try using shared memory for both A and B_T tiles: ```cuda #define TM 8 #define TN 32 #define TK 64 __shared__ half sA[TM][TK]; __shared__ half sB[TN][TK]; // Each warp loads B_T for its columns for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += TK) { // Load A tile: sA[ty][k] = A[row][k0+k] // Coalesced: threads with same ty, varying tx load consecutive k for (int k = tx; k < TK; k += blockDim.x) { if (row < M && k0 + k < K) { sA[ty][k] = A[row * K + k0 + k]; } } // Load B_T tile: sB[tx][k] = B_T[col][k0+k] // NOT coalesced because different tx have different col for (int k = ty; k < TK; k += blockDim.y) { if (idx_pos < index_size && k0 + k < K) { sB[tx][k] = B_T[col * K + k0 + k]; } } __syncthreads(); for (int k = 0; k < TK && k0 + k < K; k++) { acc += __half2float(sA[ty][k]) * __half2float(sB[tx][k]); } __syncthreads(); } ``` For the B_T load: each thread loads its own sequence, but since threads write to sB[tx][k] for different tx, there's no bank conflict. The issue is just global memory coalescing for the load itself. On H100, with independent sequential streams per thread, the global memory loads for B_T might not be too bad. The GPU can handle multiple concurrent threads with sequential access patterns. Actually, to make the B_T load more efficient, we can have each warp load B_T for just its columns. Since each warp has 32 threads with 32 different tx values, they load 32 different columns. The memory system on H100 is sophisticated and can handle this with L2 cache and memory coalescing across warps. For A, since all threads in a warp load the same row but different k values, and row-major A has consecutive elements for varying k, this is perfectly coalesced. Let me implement this with the transposed B approach. Wait, but I need to be careful about one thing. In `__init__`, if I transpose B, I need to make sure the values match. In PyTorch: ```python self.B = torch.empty(K, N, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda').uniform_(-8, 7) self.B_T = self.B.t().contiguous() # N x K, B_T[j, k] = B[k, j] ``` This should give the correct values for B_T[j, k] = original B[k, j]. Now let me verify correctness. The CPU reference in CUTLASS computes: ```cpp tensor_d_ref.at({i, b_c_d_col}) += alpha * tensor_a.at({i, k}) * tensor_b.at({k, b_c_d_col}); ``` With my row-major PyTorch and transposed B: - A[i, k] = A[i*K + k] - B[k, b_c_d_col] = original B[k, b_c_d_col] = B_T[b_c_d_col, k] (since we transposed B) - So the computation is: D[i, b_c_d_col] += alpha * A[i*K + k] * B_T[b_c_d_col * K + k] - This matches my kernel exactly! And for C and D: - C[i, b_c_d_col] = C[i*N + b_c_d_col] - D[i, b_c_d_col] = D[i*N + b_c_d_col] Wait, in the CUTLASS code with ColumnMajor, the output for C and D is stored as: - D[i, j] = D[j * M + i] (ColumnMajor M x N) But in my PyTorch row-major version: - D[i, j] = D[i * N + j] (RowMajor M x N) Does this matter? Only if we compare element-for-element at the same linear indices. But since D is M x N in both cases, and we're only writing to D[i, indices[p]] for all valid (i, p), the final D tensor should have the same logical values, just stored in different memory layouts. Actually, PyTorch doesn't distinguish between row-major and column-major in its tensor semantics - a tensor of shape (M, N) is always interpreted as row-major for indexing purposes. So `D[0, 1]` always means the element in the first row, second column, regardless of the underlying storage. But if the reference creates D as `torch.zeros(M, N)` and then does `D[:, indices] = result`, it's using row-major indexing. And my kernel writes to `D[row * N + col]`, which is correct for row-major. However, there's one more thing. In the CUTLASS code, the input/output matrices use ColumnMajor. When we copy data between host and device, or when the CPU reference computes and compares, it uses CUTLASS's tensor views which handle ColumnMajor indexing correctly. For the PyTorch evaluation, the hidden reference uses standard PyTorch tensors (row-major by default). So my kernel should use row-major indexing. Now let me think about whether transposing B in init is acceptable. The evaluation contract says: "ModelNew must define __init__(*get_init_inputs()) and forward(*get_inputs()) so outputs match the hidden reference implementation on the evaluation GPU." As long as I compute the correct output for the given inputs, I'm fine. The internal representation of B is up to me. Let me now write the corrected kernel. I'll also make sure to check if beta is used properly. Looking at the I/O contract: ```python ALPHA = 1.0 BETA = 0.0 ``` So beta is 0.0 in this case. But the kernel should handle both cases. Wait, in the CUTLASS code, the CPU reference computes: ```cpp tensor_d_ref.at({i, b_c_d_col}) += (beta * tensor_c.at({i, b_c_d_col})); ``` So if beta = 0, C is not added. If beta = 1, C is added. If there are duplicate indices in the scatter, we'd need to add C once per (i, col), but the CUTLASS code seems to only add C once per gathered index, not for all N columns. In my kernel, I only write to D[i, col] for the gathered columns. So for the non-gathered columns, D remains at its initial value. The CUTLASS code initializes D to zeros and then only computes for the gathered columns. So D for non-gathered columns should be 0. In my PyTorch kernel, I'm initializing D with `torch.zeros({M, N})`. Then I only write to `D[i*N + col]`. This matches the reference. Let me now code up the full solution with the transpose optimization. But actually, let me first try WITHOUT the transpose to see if there's a simpler fix. The previous mismatch was because I accessed A and B with wrong strides. If I access them correctly for row-major, maybe it works. Let me try a direct row-major version: - A[i, k] = A[i*K + k] - B[k, j] = B[k*N + j] - But B[k, j] for fixed j and varying k is strided by N ```cuda __global__ void kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, const int* __restrict__ indices, int M, int K, int N, int index_size, float alpha, float beta) { int row = blockIdx.x * blockDim.y + threadIdx.y; int idx_pos = blockIdx.y * blockDim.x + threadIdx.x; if (row >= M || idx_pos >= index_size) return; int col = indices[idx_pos]; float acc = 0.0f; for (int k = 0; k < K; k++) { half a = A[row * K + k]; half b = B[k * N + col]; acc += __half2float(a) * __half2float(b); } float val = alpha * acc; if (beta != 0.0f) { val += beta * C[row * N + col]; } D[row * N + col] = val; } ``` This is correct for row-major tensors, but B access is strided. Let me test this for correctness first, then optimize. Actually, I realize my first attempt had `B[col * K + gk]` which is treating B as column-major. That was wrong for row-major PyTorch. The correct access for row-major B is `B[k * N + col]`. Let me send this direct-access version and test it for correctness. If it works, I can then optimize.
